    MIN(s.s_store_id)        AS min_s_store_id,
    MIN(s.s_store_name)      AS min_s_store_name,
    MIN(ss.ss_net_profit)    AS min_ss_net_profit,
    MIN(sr.sr_net_loss)      AS min_sr_net_loss,
    MIN(cs.cs_net_profit)    AS min_cs_net_profit,
    MIN(ss.ss_item_sk)       AS min_ss_item_sk,
    MIN(sr.sr_ticket_number) AS min_sr_ticket_number,
    MIN(cs.cs_order_number)  AS min_cs_order_number
FROM store_sales AS ss
JOIN store_returns AS sr
  ON ss.ss_customer_sk   = sr.sr_customer_sk
 AND ss.ss_item_sk       = sr.sr_item_sk
 AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
JOIN catalog_sales AS cs
  ON sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 AND sr.sr_item_sk     = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN date_dim AS d1
  ON d1.d_date_sk = ss.ss_sold_date_sk
JOIN date_dim AS d2
  ON d2.d_date_sk = sr.sr_returned_date_sk
JOIN date_dim AS d3
  ON d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_sold_date_sk
JOIN store AS s
  ON s.s_store_sk = ss.ss_store_sk
JOIN item AS i
  ON i.i_item_sk = ss.ss_item_sk
WHERE d1.d_year = 2000
  AND d1.d_moy  = 6
  AND d2.d_year = 2000
  AND d2.d_moy  BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND d3.d_year = 2000
  AND d3.d_moy  BETWEEN 6 AND 8;
```

</analysis>
The original query is a TPC-DS style aggregate with no GROUP BY: it returns a single row of MIN values over the join product of store_sales, store_returns, catalog_sales, three date_dim aliases, store, and item. The plan drives from date_dim d2 (June-Aug 2000), probes store_returns, joins store_sales via the (ss_item_sk, ss_ticket_number) primary key, then matches catalog_sales by customer/item with BitmapAnd on separate indexes, finally probing d1/d3, store, and item. Main inefficiencies: (1) implicit comma joins make the join graph opaque and error-prone; (2) date_dim d2 scan uses a filter over the date_sk index because no composite (d_year, d_moy) index exists; (3) catalog_sales access combines two bitmap indexes where a single composite index on (cs_bill_customer_sk, cs_item_sk, cs_sold_date_sk) would be more selective; (4) constant expression 6+2 is trivially foldable. The MIN aggregation is preserved exactly — even when no rows qualify, both original and rewrite return one row of NULLs. Since MIN over duplicated join combinations equals MIN over participating base rows, no join explosion affects correctness, so no EXISTS/scalar-subquery decomposition is warranted at this selectivity level. A CTE for the repeated June-Aug 2000 date filter was considered but rejected: date_dim is tiny and d1 has a different filter (June only), so materialization adds overhead without reducing meaningful work.
</analysis>

Both statements are semantically equivalent rewrites of the original TPC-DS query (Q75-style). ID 0 is only a minor cosmetic cleanup — it keeps the comma-join syntax, so the optimizer must still infer join conditions, and it drops table aliases entirely (using bare column names), which is fragile if column prefixes change. ID 1 converts all joins to explicit ANSI JOIN syntax with clear table aliases, making the join graph explicit and easier for the optimizer to reorder and for humans to verify. Its WHERE clause contains only filter predicates (year, month ranges), cleanly separating filters from joins. It also adds meaningful column aliases for readability. The rewritten joins (`ss–sr` on customer/item/ticket, `sr–cs` on customer/item) faithfully preserve the original semantics, and the date filters (d1: year 2000, moy 6; d2/d3: year 2000, moy 6–8) match exactly. ID 1 is the most effective and safest rewrite.

A line-by-line comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs reveals that all three plans are structurally and numerically identical. The rewritten query introduced table aliases (sr, ss, cs, d1, d2, d3, s, i) to improve readability, and the enhanced query preserved those aliases. However, neither the rewriting nor the enhancement step produced any changes to the physical execution plan chosen by the optimizer:

- Total cost remains 35253.62..35253.63 with an estimated 1 output row and width=236 in all three plans.
- Every intermediate node keeps the same cost, row estimate, and width — including the innermost Parallel Index Scan on date_dim (cost 0.29..3133.19), the deep Nested Loop chain (six levels), the BitmapAnd combination over catalog_sales indexes, and all single-row Index Scans on date_dim, store, and item.
- Join algorithms are unchanged: a deep chain of Nested Loop joins with a single Gather node (2 workers) feeding the parallel subtree.
- No Sort, Hash, or Materialize nodes exist in any version, so there is no sorting or intermediate materialization to eliminate.
- Scan methods are unchanged: Parallel Index Scan, Index Scan, and Bitmap Heap/Bitmap Index Scans are used in exactly the same places.
- Worker count is identical (Workers Planned: 2) in all plans.
- Memory utilization patterns are identical; the only bitmap structure (BitmapAnd on catalog_sales) retains the same cost in all versions.

The conclusion is that the rewritten query was purely cosmetic (alias substitution), and the enhancement attempt did not alter plan shape, access paths, join order, or cost. The optimizer already selected an efficient index-driven plan for the original query, so the rewrite/enhancement provided no measurable performance benefit in the estimated plan.

Let's consider if there is any hidden issue: The original query has `min(i_item_id)`, `min(i_item_desc)`, etc. It uses `min` on all columns. Since there's no group by, it's a single aggregate. The use of `min` on string columns is fine. The query might return unexpected results if there are multiple rows, but that's the intended TPC-DS query. No issue.

What about the join order? The original query lists tables in order: store_sales, store_returns, catalog_sales, date_dim d1, d2, d3, store, item. The optimizer can reorder. The explain shows it reorders. So no need to change.

What about the condition `d2.d_moy between 6 and 6 + 2`? The `6 + 2` is evaluated at runtime? Actually PostgreSQL will constant-fold it. So no performance difference. But for readability, fold to 8. The previous report did that.

What about using `d_moy >= 6 AND d_moy <= 8` instead of `between`? Both are equivalent. `between` is fine.

What about the join condition `ss_customer_sk = sr_customer_sk`? In the explain, it's a filter on store_sales after index cond. That's fine.

</analysis>
The query is a single MIN aggregate over a star-style join chain (store_sales → store_returns → catalog_sales) with three date_dim dimension joins plus item and store lookups. The original plan is already efficient: it uses index scans and nested loops with a very low estimated row count (~1). Rewriting the implicit joins to explicit INNER JOINs improves readability without changing the plan. Constant folding of `6 + 2` to `8` is applied. CTEs were deliberately avoided: date_dim is small, filters are already selective, there are no redundant subquery computations, and decomposing the multi-table MIN aggregate into separate

=== Worker 1: Start Rewrite ===
 CTEs (e.g., pre-aggregating catalog_sales by customer/item) would add scan overhead without measurable benefit at these cardinalities. Given the data statistics, the most impactful further optimization would be composite covering indexes on catalog_sales and store_returns join/filter columns, but that is physical design rather than a SQL rewrite. The rewritten SQL is executable, equivalent, and as efficient</produced_sql as the original>
```sql
select.

</analysis>

The query is a single ungrouped MIN aggregate over a multi-fact join (store_sales → store_returns → catalog_sales) constrained by three date_dim filters (d1: June 2000; d2/d3: June–Aug 2000), plus dimension joins to store and item.

The existing EXPLAIN plan (cost ≈ 35253) is already index-driven and near-optimal:
- Parallel index scan on date_dim d2 → index scan of store_returns on sr_returned_date_sk (≈3.6k rows)
- Point lookups into store_sales via its primary key (ss_item_sk, ss_ticket_number)
- BitmapAnd probes on catalog_sales via customer/item indexes
- PK joins to d1, d3, store, item

Potential further rewrites were evaluated:
1. CTE pre-filtering of the fact tables: rejected — materializing ss (~480k rows), sr (~360k rows), cs (~720k rows) would forfeit the nested-loop index advantage and likely force expensive hash joins.
2. EXISTS-style date filtering: rejected — equivalent but the optimizer already flattens these; no plan change, possible regression.
3. Join reorder / explicit join order hints: unnecessary — the optimizer already picks the best order.

The only meaningful further improvement is schema-level covering indexes (e.g., store_sales(ss_item_sk, ss_ticket_number) INCLUDE (ss_customer_sk, ss_net_profit, ss_store_sk, ss_sold_date_sk), and catalog_sales(cs_bill_customer_sk, cs_item_sk) INCLUDE (cs_sold_date_sk, cs_net_profit, cs_order_number)), which are outside the scope of SQL rewriting. The SQL itself is already well-optimized; the rewrite above applies constant folding and explicit-join readability improvements while remaining fully equivalent.
</analysis>

Both enhanced SQL statements are semantically equivalent and correctly convert the implicit comma-join syntax into explicit ANSI JOINs, which improves readability and helps the optimizer. The difference between them is minor:

- Statement 0 uses table aliases (ss, sr, cs, s, i) and fully qualifies every column, making the query unambiguous, self-documenting, and less prone to errors if columns with the same name exist across tables. It also pre-computes `6 + 2` as `8`, simplifying the predicate.
- Statement 1 omits aliases and relies on unqualified column names, which works but is less robust and less clear.

Since both would produce identical execution plans in most modern optimizers, the choice comes down to clarity and maintainability. Statement 0's explicit aliasing and qualified column references make it the most professional and least error-prone version.

1. Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change percentage: 0.00%. All three plans produce an identical top-level cost of 35253.62..35253.63 for the final Aggregate node. The rewriting (alias introduction) and the enhancement pass did not trigger any re-planning or cost improvement — the optimizer selected exactly the same plan in all three cases.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: 0.00%. The dominant cost node in all three plans is the innermost parallel Nested Loop feeding the Gather node (cost=1.16..34224.04 per worker, aggregating to ~35224.14 at the Gather), which remains unchanged across all versions. No node's cost increased or decreased.

2. Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: None. The plan already employs a highly index-driven strategy in all three versions: a Parallel Index Scan on date_dim (d2), Index Scans on store_returns (via idx_store_returns_returned_date) and store_sales (via store_sales_pkey), a Bitmap Heap Scan on catalog_sales backed by a BitmapAnd of two Bitmap Index Scans, and Index Scans on date_dim (d1, d3), store, and item. No Seq Scan appears anywhere, and no scan method transition occurred between versions.
- Join algorithm refinements: None. All joins remain Nested Loops in every version. Given the highly selective (rows=1) estimates at each join level, Nested Loop is the appropriate algorithm, and the rewriter/enhancer did not (nor needed to) convert any join to Hash Join or Merge Join.
- Elimination of explicit sorting and reduction of intermediate result sets: Already absent in the original plan — no Sort nodes exist in any version, as the index-driven Nested Loop pipeline produces rows in a usable order and the final node is a simple Aggregate with rows=1. Intermediate result set cardinalities (3650 rows from the store_returns/store_sales join, 1 row at each subsequent level) are identical across all three plans, so no reduction was achieved.

3. Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: Unchanged. There are no Hash nodes requiring work_mem in any version; the only memory-sensitive structures are the small Bitmap Heap/BitmapAnd scans on catalog_sales (cost=10.81), which remain identical. Buffer behavior is therefore expected to be equivalent across all three executions.
- Worker count adjustment: Unchanged. All three plans retain the same parallel structure: a Gather node with Workers Planned: 2 over the innermost Nested Loop subtree. No increase or decrease in parallelism was introduced.

4. Other Improvements:
- Readability/semantic clarity: The rewritten and enhanced versions introduce explicit table aliases (sr, ss, cs, d1, d2, d3, s, i), making join conditions unambiguous (e.g., "ss.ss_customer_sk = cs_bill_customer_sk" instead of "store_sales.ss_customer_sk = cs_bill_customer_sk"). This improves SQL readability and maintainability without affecting execution.
- Practical takeaway: The query was already well-optimized from the original plan's perspective — fully index-supported access paths, parallelism on the selective date-filtered driver branch, and no sorting or spilling. The rewrite/enhancement pass was effectively a no-op for performance.
- Residual optimization opportunities (not realized in any version): the estimated cost is dominated by the innermost loop (~34224 per worker) driven by the date_dim d2 → store_returns → store_sales chain with an estimated 3650 intermediate rows; further gains would likely require schema-level changes (e.g., a composite index on store_returns(sr_returned_date_sk, sr_item_sk, sr_ticket_number, sr_customer_sk) to enable index-only scans, or improved statistics) rather than query rewriting. Additionally, the repeated Filter on date_dim nodes (d_moy/d_year applied after Index Cond) could be avoided with a functional or partial index on (d_date_sk) WHERE d_year = 2000 AND d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8.

That would be similar original uses ` to the original plan6 +: for 2` which is fine each date. Our SQL_sk in is standard subquery, index.

Now scan store_returns., final response So the plan. might be identical to original, just without the date_dim join node. The cost might be slightly lower because it avoids the date_dim index scan? But the date_dim index scan was already there. Actually, original plan had d2 index scan as the outer, then store_returns index scan. In the new plan, the subquery is materialized once, then for each date_sk, index scan store_returns. That's essentially the same, but the subquery materialization might be cheaper than the parallel index scan on date_dim? It's negligible. So the plan might be nearly identical. The benefit is marginal.

What about d1 and d3? In original, they are nested loop index scans on date_dim after the fact joins. In new plan, they become semi-joins. The planner might choose to apply them as filters on the fact tables before other joins, or as hash semi-joins. Since the date_sk sets are small, it could hash them and do a quick check per row. This avoids the index scan on date_dim per row. That could be a win if there are many candidate rows. In the original plan, d1 and d3 are index scans on date_dim for each row that reaches that point. If there are, say, 1000 rows reaching that point, that's 1000 index scans. A hash lookup would be faster. So the semi-join rewrite could improve performance by replacing nested loop index probes with hash lookups. The planner might choose hash semi-join if it estimates the outer side is large. But it might also choose nested loop with index on date_dim because the date_dim is small and index is fast. It depends on cost. We can't know without running. But it's a reasonable optimization to try.

Another optimization: Use `EXISTS` instead of `IN` to avoid potential issues with NULLs. But d_date_sk is primary key, non-null. IN is fine.

Could we use a CTE to compute the date_sk sets once and reuse? The d2 and d3 filters are identical: both `d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8 AND d_year=2000`. So we can compute the date_sk set for Jun-Aug 2000 once and use it for both `sr_returned_date_sk` and `cs_sold_date_sk`. The original query has two separate date_dim joins d2 and d3 with identical filters. We can replace both with a single CTE or subquery. That reduces the date_dim scans from two to one. This is a good optimization! Let's check: d2 filters on sr_returned_date_sk; d3 filters on cs_sold_date_sk. Both have the exact same condition: `d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8 AND d_year=2000`. So we can compute the set of date_sk for Jun-Aug 2000 once and use it for both. This is a clear redundancy. The original query joins date_dim twice with the same filter. We can eliminate one scan by using a CTE:
```sql
WITH date_range AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8
      AND d_year = 2000
),
june_2000 AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_moy = 6
      AND d_year = 2000
)
SELECT ...
FROM store_sales ss
JOIN store_returns sr ON ...
JOIN catalog_sales cs ON ...
JOIN store s ON ...
JOIN item i ON ...
WHERE ss.ss_sold_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM june_2000)
  AND sr.sr_returned_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_range)
  AND cs.cs_sold_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_range);
```
This computes the Jun-Aug date set once. But will the planner materialize the CTE and reuse it? In Postgres, a non-recursive CTE referenced multiple times is usually materialized by default (unless `NOT MATERIALIZED`). So it will compute the date_range once and reuse it for both IN subqueries. This could be more efficient than two separate date_dim scans. However, the date_dim is small (73049 rows), so scanning it twice is cheap. But still, it's a reduction. The main benefit is that the planner can hash the date_range and use it for both semi-joins. This is a good rewrite.

Can we also combine the `june_2000` with `date_range`? June 2000 is a subset of Jun-Aug 2000? Yes, June is within Jun-Aug. But the condition for d1 is `d_moy = 6 AND d_year = 2000`. This is a subset of `d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8 AND d_year=2000`. We could use the `date_range` for d1 as well? No, because d1 requires only June, not July and August. If we used the broader range for d1, we would include July and August sales, which would change the result. So we need a separate set

 for June. But we could derive June from date_range by filtering `d_moy = 6` if we include d_moy in the CTE. For example:
```sql
WITH dates AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk, d_moy
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2000
      AND d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8
)
SELECT ...
FROM ...
WHERE ss.ss_sold_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM dates WHERE d_moy = 6)
  AND sr.sr_returned_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM dates)
  AND cs.cs_sold_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM dates);
```
This computes the date_dim scan once for the three filters. The `dates` CTE has ~91 rows. Then the June filter is a filter on this small set. This is even better: one date_dim scan instead of three. The original query joins date_dim three times, each time scanning/filtering. In the plan, d2 is scanned as the driving table, then d1 and d3 are index lookups. If we replace with a single CTE, the planner can materialize the 91 date_sks once, then use them for semi-joins. The June set is a subset of 31 date_sks. This could reduce the date_dim access to a single scan. Since date_dim is small, the benefit is small, but it's a clean optimization. It also simplifies the query.

WITH date_filter AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk, d_moy
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2000
      AND d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8

SELECT MIN(i.i_item_id)         AS min_i_item_id,
       MIN(i.i_item_desc)       AS min_i_item_desc,
       MIN(s.s_store_id)        AS min_s_store_id,
       MIN(s.s_store_name)      AS min_s_store_name,
       MIN(ss.ss_net_profit)    AS min_ss_net_profit,
       MIN(sr.sr_net_loss)      AS min_sr_net_loss,
       MIN(cs.cs_net_profit)    AS min_cs_net_profit,
       MIN(ss.ss_item_sk)       AS min_ss_item_sk,
       MIN(sr.sr_ticket_number) AS min_sr_ticket_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number)  AS min_cs_order_number
FROM store_sales ss
JOIN store_returns sr
  ON ss.ss_customer_sk  = sr.sr_customer_sk
 AND ss.ss_item_sk      = sr.sr_item_sk
 AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
JOIN catalog_sales cs
  ON sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 AND sr.sr_item_sk     = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN store s
  ON ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
JOIN item i
  ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
WHERE ss.ss_sold_date_sk IN (
        SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_filter WHERE d_moy = 6
      )
  AND sr.sr_returned_date_sk IN (
        SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_filter
      )

        SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_filter

    },
    {
        "group": "Subquery_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Replaced the three inner joins to `date_dim` (d1, d2, d3) with semi-joins (`IN` subqueries), since no `date_dim` columns appear in the SELECT list and `d_date_sk` is unique. This converts one-to-one filter joins into semi-joins the optimizer can execute as hash semi-joins, avoiding per-row nested-loop index probes on date_dim."
    },
    {
        "group": "Query_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Consolidated the two identical date_dim filters (d2 and d3: `d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8 AND d_year = 2000`) plus the June-only filter (d1: `d_moy = 6 AND d_year = 2000`) into a single CTE `date_filter` computed once over the small date_dim table (73049 rows). The June subset is derived by filtering `d_moy = 6` on the CTE, avoiding three separate date_dim scans/joins. Because the CTE is referenced three times, PostgreSQL will materialize it once."
    },
    {
        "group": "Join_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Rewrote implicit comma-style joins to explicit ANSI INNER JOINs with join predicates in ON clauses, improving join-order readability and letting the planner optimize the fact-table chain (store_sales → store_returns → catalog_sales) with dimension filters expressed as semi-joins rather than extra join nodes."
    },

        "group": "Constant_folding",
        "produced_suggestion": "Pre-computed the literal arithmetic `6 + 2 = 8` so range predicates are directly sargable."

</analysis>
The original query joins nine tables, including three separate joins to `date_dim` (d1, d2, d3), even though no `date_dim` column is selected — the dimension tables serve purely as filters. In the observed plan, d2 drives the store_returns access via the `sr_returned_date_sk` index, but d1 and d3 are applied as nested-loop index probes AFTER the expensive fact-table joins (d1 is applied after the catalog_sales lookup). This wastes work: rows that fail the d1 (June-only) or d3 (Jun–Aug) date checks still incur catalog_sales/store/item lookups first.

The rewrite makes three improvements while preserving exact equivalence:
1. Since `date_dim.d_date_sk` is a unique key, an inner join with date filters is equivalent to a semi-join: `fact.date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_dim WHERE ...)`. This removes three join nodes from the plan and lets the optimizer apply date filters earlier / as hash semi-joins instead of per-row nested-loop probes.
2. d2 and d3 have identical predicates (`d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8 AND d_year = 2000`), and d1 (`d_moy = 6 AND d_year = 2000`) is a subset of that same year-range. A single small CTE `date_filter` (≈91 rows given date_dim has 73,049 rows spanning ~200 years) is computed once and reused three times, reducing redundant date_dim access. The June-only restriction for store_sales is maintained as a filter on the CTE, keeping the filter condition semantics intact in the main query.
3. Explicit ANSI joins plus constant folding (`6 + 2` → `8`) clean up the predicate structure.

Data statistics support this: date_dim is tiny (73,049 rows; ~31 rows for June 2000, ~91 for Jun–Aug 2000), so materializing and hashing the CTE is essentially free, while the fact tables are large (store_sales 28.8M, store_returns 7.2M, catalog_sales 14.4M) — pushing the date filters down as semi-joins before/alongside the fact joins avoids unnecessary catalog_sales and item/store lookups for rows failing the date tests. Additional physical-design recommendations (not query rewrites): composite indexes on `store_sales(ss_item_sk, ss_ticket_number, ss_customer_sk)`, `catalog_sales(cs_bill_customer_sk, cs_item_sk)`, and `store_returns(sr_returned_date_sk, sr_customer_sk, sr_item_sk, sr_ticket_number)` would further reduce recheck/filter overhead.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
WITH date_filter AS (
    SELECT d_date_sk, d_moy
    FROM date_dim
    WHERE d_year = 2000
      AND d_moy BETWEEN 6 AND 8
)
SELECT MIN(i.i_item_id)         AS min_i_item_id,
       MIN(i.i_item_desc)       AS min_i_item_desc,
       MIN(s.s_store_id)        AS min_s_store_id,
       MIN(s.s_store_name)      AS min_s_store_name,
       MIN(ss.ss_net_profit)    AS min_ss_net_profit,
       MIN(sr.sr_net_loss)      AS min_sr_net_loss,
       MIN(cs.cs_net_profit)    AS min_cs_net_profit,
       MIN(ss.ss_item_sk)       AS min_ss_item_sk,
       MIN(sr.sr_ticket_number) AS min_sr_ticket_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number)  AS min_cs_order_number
FROM store_sales ss
JOIN store_returns sr
  ON ss.ss_customer_sk  = sr.sr_customer_sk
 AND ss.ss_item_sk      = sr.sr_item_sk
 AND ss.ss_ticket_number = sr.sr_ticket_number
JOIN catalog_sales cs
  ON sr.sr_customer_sk = cs.cs_bill_customer_sk
 AND sr.sr_item_sk     = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN store s
  ON ss.ss_store_sk = s.s_store_sk
JOIN item i
  ON ss.ss_item_sk = i.i_item_sk
WHERE ss.ss_sold_date_sk IN (
        SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_filter WHERE d_moy = 6
      )
  AND sr.sr_returned_date_sk IN (
        SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_filter
      )
  AND cs.cs_sold_date_sk IN (
        SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_filter
      )
```

Enhanced SQL 0 is a faithful and efficient rewrite. It converts the comma joins into explicit INNER JOINs with the join predicates placed in ON clauses, preserving all original semantics (d1 filter on June 2000, d2/d3 filters on June–August 2000). This gives the optimizer clear join ordering freedom and allows hash joins with dimension-table filtering pushed down early. 

Enhanced SQL 1, while structurally organized with a CTE, uses `IN (SELECT ...)` subqueries for date filtering. Although many optimizers can semi-join rewrite these, they add unnecessary complexity and risk worse plans compared to direct joins on date_dim, and the extra `WHERE d_moy = 6` subquery fragment adds little value. Statement 0 is simpler, semantically identical, and most likely to produce an optimal execution plan.

Every node type, every cost figure, every estimated row count, and every index usage is exactly the same across the three plans.

Key observations supporting this conclusion:

1. The total plan cost is identical in all three cases: the top-level Aggregate node shows cost=35253.62..35253.63, and the underlying Nested Loop shows cost=1012.99..35253.59.

2. The plan shape is unchanged: a deep chain of seven Nested Loop joins under a final Aggregate, with a parallel Gather node (2 workers) feeding a Nested Loop that combines a Parallel Index Scan on date_dim with Index Scans on store_returns and store_sales.

3. All auxiliary structures are preserved: the BitmapAnd node with two Bitmap Index Scans on catalog_sales (idx_catalog_sales_bill_customer and idx_catalog_sales_item), the Index Scans on date_dim d1 and d3, and the lookups on store (idx_store_sk) and item (idx_item_sk) appear identically in all three outputs.

4. Estimated row counts (e.g., 3650 rows from the store_returns join, 1 row at each upper level) and row widths are unchanged.

Because there is zero deviation between the three plans, no performance improvement (or regression) can be attributed to the rewriting or enhancement steps at the optimizer level. The rewrite evidently did not alter the query semantics in a way that would trigger a different plan choice by the PostgreSQL planner — the planner converged on the exact same access paths and join ordering in all cases. Consequently, the analysis below reports 0% change across all dimensions, and the "improvements" section notes that any benefit of the rewrite would have to come from factors outside the EXPLAIN cost model (e.g., runtime statistics differences, plan-time vs. execution-time behavior) — none of which are visible here.
